## 1. [The NIVARIA-Local Group simulations: Unveiling the origin of rotation curve diversity in dwarf galaxies and its connection to galaxy morphology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15071) <a id="link1"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15071

**Authors:** A. Contreras-Santos, A. Di Cintio, A. Negri, J. Sarrato-Alós, S. Cardona-Barrero, G. García-Bethencourt, C. Brook, N. I. Libeskind, A. Knebe, A. Macciò

**Abstract:** Dwarf galaxies exhibit a large diversity in rotation curve shapes, challenging galaxy formation models within the $Λ$CDM paradigm. We present NIVARIA-LG, a new suite of cosmological simulations studying dwarf galaxy evolution in realistic Local Group environments. This intermediate-resolution pilot lays the groundwork for the full-scale NIVARIA project. The simulations combine constrained Local Group initial conditions from HESTIA with the NIHAO galaxy formation model. The simulated galaxies reproduce observed stellar-to-halo mass relations, match the Baryonic Tully-Fisher relation, and capture the distribution of stellar effective radii, including compact and diffuse galaxies. We analyse the structural and kinematic properties of isolated dwarf galaxies with $10^7 \lesssim M_\star/M_\odot \lesssim 10^{10}$, focusing on rotation curve diversity. NIVARIA-LG naturally produces a broad range of rotation curve shapes, including slowly rising systems underrepresented in previous simulations and steep rotation curves. We find that rotation curve diversity is primarily associated with gas extent and inner dark matter structure: more extended gas and shallower inner density profiles correspond to more slowly rising curves, while stellar effective radius shows little independent association. Individual galaxies show that rotation curve shapes change rapidly as gas is redistributed during assembly. Recent accretion events are also associated with more slowly rising curves in the most massive dwarfs. Together, these results suggest that rotation curve diversity is linked to the baryonic cycle, which shapes the inner dark matter distribution. The Local Group environment and its filaments influence this evolution by regulating gas accretion and redistribution, producing the observed diversity in rotation curve shapes.

**Comment:** Dwarf galaxy simulations, rotation curve diversity, inner dark matter profiles

## 2. [Environmental Dependence of Satellite Planarity and Orbital-Pole Coherence around Milky Way Analogues in a Hydrodynamical Cosmological Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16710) <a id="link2"></a>

**Authors:** Peng Wang, Yu Luo, Jiang Chang, Xi Kang, Shi Shao

**Abstract:** We examine whether the unusual spatial flattening and orbital-plane coherence of the Milky Way's classical satellites are connected to the Galaxy's location in the cosmic web. We compare the 11 classical satellites with the 11 most massive satellites of 11,922 Milky Way-mass hosts in the TNG300-1 hydrodynamical simulation. We measure flattening with both the simple inertia tensor (SIT), which retains radial information, and the reduced inertia tensor (RIT), which isolates angular anisotropy. Orbital-plane coherence is characterized by the minimum opening angle containing the axes of the eight most coplanar satellite orbits. For the Milky Way, we find $(c/a,b/a)=(0.183,0.558)$ for SIT and $(0.364,0.736)$ for RIT, together with an eight-orbit opening angle of $21.9^\circ$. The corresponding fractions of simulated systems at least as extreme are $4.94\%$, $12.28\%$, and $5.04\%$, respectively. We describe the environment using the velocity-shear tensor smoothed on $2\,h^{-1}\mathrm{Mpc}$ and compare the full sample with Local Group-like pairs, systems whose shear eigenvalues match those reconstructed for the Local Group, and hosts in knots, filaments, sheets, and voids. Among the well-sampled environmental subsets, the shape and opening-angle distributions differ only weakly. Although the cosmic web imprints preferred orientations on host spins and satellite orbital-plane axes, the Milky Way combines orientations that are uncommon in the simulation. We conclude that the present-day cosmic-web environment alone does not substantially increase the occurrence of Milky Way-like satellite configurations. This result does not rule out an origin in anisotropic accretion, since neither the present-day web class nor its shear eigenvalues fully encodes the assembly history of an individual satellite system.

Because the absorption is detected against the central UV or optical continuum, the gas must lie in the foreground of the star-forming region and is moving away from the observer and toward the background UV source in projection. A VLA--THINGS HI spectrum extracted at the COS position within one VLA beam also shows a redshifted neutral component at $v_{\rm hel}=329.2\pm0.9\ {\rm km\ s^{-1}}$, offset by $38.2\pm1.3\ {\rm km\ s^{-1}}$ from systemic and by $27.6\pm1.0\ {\rm km\ s^{-1}}$ from the dominant local HI component. Together, the redshifted UV absorption, the redshifted HI component, and the disturbed large-scale HI velocity field show that the central anomalous cool gas is observed in a galaxy with a disturbed extended neutral disk. ## 4. [Self-Interacting Dark Matter: From Particle Physics Theories to Structure Formation Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16968) <a id="link4"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16968

**Authors:** Martin Rosenlyst, Vinh Tran, Oliver Zier, Xuejian Shen, Mark Vogelsberger, Thejs Brinckmann, Mads T. Frandsen, Jesús Zavala, Frederik Holt Dihver Christensen, Mikkel Theiss Kristensen

**Abstract:** We establish a unified theory-to-simulation framework for self-interacting dark matter (SIDM) that integrates three components: (i) a comprehensive, self-contained treatment of its particle-physics foundations and a broad range of model classes; (ii) a systematic, quantitative mapping from microscopic dark-sector physics to structure-formation simulation inputs through explicit expressions and numerical prescriptions; and (iii) the development, implementation, and validation of a broad suite of SIDM algorithms and a dedicated DM decay module in the moving-mesh code AREPO-2. Under (i), we detail the particle content, symmetries, interactions, production mechanisms, and constraints of a broad landscape of elementary and composite SIDM models, and present representative viable benchmark models. Under (ii), the mapping specifies cosmological initial conditions, internal-state masses and populations, reaction networks, velocity- and angle-dependent cross sections, dissipative energy loss, and decay rates and recoil kinematics. We then apply this mapping to a broad range of SIDM model classes. Under (iii), we implement and validate SIDM algorithms with constant and velocity-dependent cross sections, covering isotropic and anisotropic scattering, multi-state elastic and inelastic reactions, and dissipative processes, alongside a dedicated DM decay module in AREPO-2. We validate these implementations through beam-lattice deflection experiments, thermalization and uniform-box decay tests, isolated-halo gravothermal core-collapse simulations, and cosmological box and zoom-in simulations. These tests recover analytical predictions, establish agreement across numerical schemes, and reproduce published benchmarks. Our theory-to-simulation framework serves as a cross-disciplinary reference and a practical bridge between SIDM particle physics and simulations of nonlinear structure formation.

**Comment:** SIDM simulations of structure formation, core-cusp relevant

**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 8

Using the position of \thestar\ in the [Mg/Mn]--[Al/Fe] plane, the abundance pattern of both the light and heavy elements, and the kinematics, we conclude that J0010-1735 most plausibly originated in a now-disrupted ultra-faint dwarf galaxy that was accreted into the MW halo. ## 6. [Untangling Similarities and Differences in Star Formation Histories Across Cosmological Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17359) <a id="link6"></a>

**Abstract:** How galaxies assemble depends on processes ranging from small-scale baryonic physics to large-scale cosmological assembly, which are difficult to disentangle with observations alone. Using non-linear dimensionality reduction (UMAP), we find that sSFH shape variation organizes along a few smooth axes in a 2D embedding, broadly corresponding to (1) peak timing, (2) peak sharpness, and (3) subsequent quenching rate. We identify sSFH shapes that many simulations share ("common"), well described to first order by dark matter halo assembly, and a few tied to individual simulations ("unique"), which likely reflect subgrid and parametrization choices such as merger-triggered burst prescriptions. A notable exception is a common population of rapidly quenched galaxies found across most simulations, likely driven by e.g., active galactic nucleus (AGN) feedback, that halo assembly alone cannot reproduce. The origin of the most distinctive unique shape, an Illustris-dominated history with an early peak and slow decline, remains unclear, but we test the effects of short-term variability. With a multi-objective auto-encoder neural network, we recover the simulation that a given sSFH is drawn from with $\sim$95\% out-of-sample accuracy; ablation tests indicate this information is embedded in long-timescale sSFH morphology rather than short-term variability. These results provide avenues for theoretical and observational follow-up connecting star formation histories to subgrid physical models.

**Abstract:** Population III (Pop III) stars link the chemical enrichment of the early Universe to the formation of the first massive black holes, yet their evolution remains poorly understood at extreme masses. In the fiducial $1000\,M_\odot$ model, convection and rotational transport carry freshly synthesized C and O into H burning layers, producing $62.1\,M_\odot$ of primary N by He exhaustion. Surface mass loss removes $20.2\,M_\odot$, including $1.8\times10^{-3}\,M_\odot$ of N, with $\log(\mathrm{N}/\mathrm{O})=0.68$, $\log(\mathrm{N}/\mathrm{C})=1.48$, and $\log(\mathrm{C}/\mathrm{O})=-0.80$. By the final hydrostatic model, the inner $100\,M_\odot$ is confined within $0.088\,R_\odot$ while the photosphere remains several thousand solar radii away. As C, Ne, O, and Si burning accelerate, chemical mixing freezes out and advanced products remain buried beneath the envelope. At core Si exhaustion, the central angular velocity reaches $4\times10^{-3}\,{\rm s^{-1}}$ while the surface rotates at $5.25\times10^{-8}\,{\rm s^{-1}}$, a contrast of $7.6\times10^4$. Contraction drives this spin up, while inner specific angular momentum changes by less than $1\%$. The maximum $j_{\rm eq}/j_{\rm ISCO}$ is $0.313$, limiting disk formation in our inside out estimate. These models show that Pop~III stars can produce strong primary N signatures and rapidly rotating black hole progenitors while their envelopes determine which products enrich the early Universe.

## 8. [Magnetic field evolution in feedback-dominated galaxies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17216) <a id="link8"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17216

**Authors:** Yue Samuel Lu, Dušan Kereš, Philip F. Hopkins, Sam B. Ponnada

**Abstract:** Over the last decade, magnetic fields (B-fields) have become a common ingredient in galaxy formation simulations. Although seemingly unimportant in matching observational constraints such as the $M_*-M_{\rm halo}$ relations, the B-field influences the circumgalactic medium (CGM) gas and determines cosmic ray (CR) propagation, making it an indispensable non-thermal component. Nevertheless, the intricate physics of B-fields, particularly how they evolve to the observed $μ$G-level field, remains largely elusive. In this work, we systematically address the evolution of B-fields over cosmic time using the FIRE-2 (Feedback In Realistic Environments) magnetohydrodynamics (MHD) simulations. We present results for both Milky Way (MW)-mass and dwarf galaxies, with and without CRs. We find that CRs have little impact on B-field properties, while the halo mass controls the saturated B-field strength at $z\sim 0$. However, all runs reach a halo-independent magnetic-to-turbulent ratio of $f_{\rm B}\sim 1-10\%$, though the methods used to measure turbulence could affect this value. We identify a three-stage evolutionary course for the B-field in tandem with galaxy formation: i. expansion, ii. amplification, and iii. saturation. The feature is universal, though the timings depend on the initial location and star formation history of the gas. Flux-freezing is the predominant driver for the first and last stages, exhibiting power relations $B\sim ρ^{2/3}$ and $ρ^{1/2}$, although the latter could also be explained by dynamical and energetic equilibria. The intermediate amplification stage, potentially driven by ``dynamos'', is fuelled by structure formation, feedback, and gas turbulence. We conjecture a numerically triggered, turbulence-driven amplification process to explain this phenomenon.

**Comment:** Galaxy simulations with new physics, CGM magnetic fields

The neutron-capture abundances in Sculptor and Draco can also be fit by models with a longer neutron star merger minimum delay time combined with a prompt r-process source that operates on the same timescale as massive star evolution, such as magneto-rotational supernovae. Additionally, the Sr and Y abundances in the dSphs set limits on the Sr and Y yields per r-process event. We also study the dustiest galaxies in our sample, showing that they are preferentially more massive and strongly star-forming relative to the parent sample, residing on the high-mass end of the star-forming main-sequence. The slope of the attenuation curve has a strong dependence on Av, varying from steep to shallow as the dust attenuation increases and the specific star-formation rate decreases. **Authors:** Lurdes Ondaro-Mallea, Boryana Hadzhiyska, Raul E. Angulo

**Abstract:** The kinetic Sunyaev-Zel'dovich (kSZ) signal stacked around a galaxy sample traces the gas around halos and is a powerful probe of baryonic feedback. Its interpretation, however, depends on the satellite galaxies in the sample. Using the FLAMINGO hydrodynamical simulations and DESI-like samples of luminous red galaxies (LRGs), bright galaxies survey (BGS), and emission-line galaxies (ELGs), we show that satellites and feedback have similar effects: both suppress the small-scale signal. This degeneracy arises because stacking on satellites, which are offset from the centre of their host haloes, mimics the extended gas distribution produced by feedback. Although satellites make up only 20-30% of each sample, they account for 50-80% of the signal because they occupy more massive halos than centrals; thus their imprint on the stacked profiles can be as large as that of feedback. At the precision of current DESI$\times$ACT data, neglecting satellites in the modelling biases the inferred gas fractions low, by up to a factor of two, and overestimates the feedback strength, particularly for the LRG and BGS samples. Remaining agnostic about the satellite population avoids this bias but reduces the constraining power, so robust constraints would require knowing the satellite fraction to better than 10%, for instance from lensing and clustering data of the same galaxy samples. Finally, a further contribution from satellites will become statistically significant with upcoming surveys: satellites trace the anisotropic gas distribution around their host haloes, an effect that contributes 10% of the signal, and will likely require simulation-based models to capture.

We analyzed eROSITA/SRG data from the four all-sky surveys (eRASS:4) for a blind, volume-limited sample of 140 galaxies within 25 Mpc from the Updated Nearby Galaxy Catalog (Karachentsev et al. 2013), cross-matched with HECATEv2 (Kyritsis et al. 2026b). We modeled the integrated X-ray spectra with thermal plasma and power-law components, stacked spectra for each subsample, and derived total and thermal X-ray luminosities. The stacked spectra show a stronger soft thermal component at high sSFR, whereas low-sSFR and early-type galaxies are increasingly dominated by the harder non-thermal continuum. For star-forming galaxies, the total 0.5-8 keV luminosity follows $\log L_X \sim 39.5 + 0.9\log\mathrm{SFR}$, with an intrinsic scatter of about 0.9 dex. The 0.5-2 keV thermal luminosity follows $\log L_X \sim 38.7 + 1.1\log\mathrm{SFR}$, with an intrinsic scatter of about 0.7 dex. The thermal luminosity remains well correlated with SFR down to $\sim 10^{-5}\,\mathrm{M}_\odot\,\mathrm{yr}^{-1}$ and is consistent with previous relations for star-forming galaxies. By contrast, the flatter and more scattered total X-ray luminosity-SFR relation suggests that unresolved X-ray binaries likely dominate at low SFR, while thermal X-ray emission remains a robust tracer of stellar feedback.

## 17. [A Comprehensive JWST Slitless Spectroscopic Census of $\sim$12,000 Emission-Line Galaxies at Redshift $0\sim9$ in COSMOS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13920) <a id="link17"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.13920

**Authors:** Shuqi Fu, Zijian Zhang, Danyang Jiang, Jie Chen, Linhua Jiang, Chenxi Bao, Yihe Wang, Ronaldo Laishram

**Abstract:** Emission-line galaxies provide key diagnostics of star formation, chemical enrichment, and AGN activity across cosmic time. We present a systematic census of emission-line sources using JWST/NIRCam wide-field slitless spectroscopy (WFSS) from the COSMOS-3D program, which covers a large contiguous area of 0.33 deg$^2$ and provides F444W grism spectroscopy over $3.9$--$5.1\ μ$m. By combining slitless spectroscopy with deep multi-band imaging data, we construct a homogeneous spectroscopic sample of 12,275 objects at redshift 0--9. The typical $3σ$ line-flux limit reaches $3.2\times10^{-18}\ {\rm erg\ s^{-1}\ cm^{-2}}$. To address source confusion inherent to slitless spectroscopy, we develop a multi-step identification framework that combines information from spectra, images, spectral energy distributions, and spatial distributions. We introduce convolutional neural network and large language model to emulate the human identification process and improve efficiency. The resulting catalog provides spectroscopic redshifts, emission-line measurements, and derived physical properties for all sources. The F444W WFSS enables the detection of multiple emission lines, including primarily [O III], H$α$, He I $\lambda10830$, [S III] $λ\lambda9531,9069$, Pa$β$, Pa$α$, Br$β$, PAH, Br$α$, allowing a continuous census of galaxies across cosmic time. We construct composite spectra in several redshift bins and detect a variety of faint spectral features that are difficult to identify in individual spectra, including higher-order hydrogen recombination lines, CO absorption bands, and [Fe II] lines. This catalog provides a key reference dataset for studies of galaxy evolution and AGN activity, and demonstrates the power of WFSS for constructing large, unbiased spectroscopic samples over wide areas. The full catalog will be publicly available online upon publication.

## 18. [An asteroseismic age estimate for the Pleiades using a neural-network emulator of rotating $δ$ Scuti pulsation models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15939) <a id="link18"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15939

**Authors:** Anuj Gautam, Simon J. Murphy

**Abstract:** Asteroseismology of $δ$ Sct stars has strong potential for determining the ages of young stars through individual mode fitting. We present a neural-network emulator trained on approximately 46 million rotating pulsation models. From stellar mass, metallicity, age, and equatorial rotation velocity, it predicts 12 classical observables, two asteroseismic scalars, and the p mode, f mode and low-order g mode frequencies for degrees $\ell=0$-3, including the individual $m$ components of rotationally split multiplets. We couple the emulator to nested sampling through a multivariate likelihood that propagates correlated emulator residuals, making full Bayesian posterior exploration practical. Applied to five $δ$ Sct stars in the Pleiades observed by TESS, the framework gives mutually consistent individual asteroseismic ages of 107-117 Myr. Combining their age posteriors gives a cluster age of $113.3\pm4.5$ Myr (statistical), consistent with the $112 \pm 5$ Myr lithium-depletion-boundary age and the 110-160 Myr range from rotating-isochrone fitting. Together with spectroscopic $v\sin i$, the inferred equatorial velocities also provide post-fit inclination estimates. Separate perturbed-grid tests using 100 matched synthetic pairs per input-physics variation give median absolute age shifts of $2.1$, $2.3$, and $5.2$ Myr for $|Δf_{\rm ov}|=0.005$, $|Δα_{\rm MLT}|=0.1$, and $|ΔY|=0.02$, respectively; the corresponding 90th percentile shifts are $6.5$, $7.6$, and $16.2$ Myr. These tests describe conditional population sensitivities, not a systematic uncertainty on the Pleiades age. Together, the cluster-age agreement and the scale of these input-physics sensitivities support $δ$ Sct asteroseismology as a complementary age estimation method for young stars.

## 19. [Where Cluster Galaxies Reside and How Strongly They Lens](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14167) <a id="link19"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14167

**Authors:** Isaque Dutra, Priyamvada Natarajan, Cian Roche

**Abstract:** The efficiency of galaxy-galaxy strong lensing (GGSL) in clusters depends jointly on the inner mass distributions of member galaxies and their locations within the cluster potential. Observed GGSL probabilities exceed $Λ$CDM predictions by nearly an order of magnitude, but the relative roles of internal structure and environment remain unclear. We disentangle these effects by comparing HST strong-lensing clusters with TNG-Cluster analogs at similar lens redshifts, matched by their primary Einstein radius. For each member, we measure its lensing strength in isolation and when embedded in the cluster field. Members in cluster-critical regions are classified as Major or Minor Perturbers depending on whether they strongly lens in isolation; those in subcritical regions are classified as Intrinsic Lenses, Boosted Lenses, or Inert Members according to whether they lens independently, only with environmental assistance, or not at all. Observed clusters contain 33.7% of selected members in cluster-critical regions, compared with 21.6% in TNG-Cluster, while their Major-to-Minor ratios are similar. Outside these regions, observations contain substantially more Intrinsic and Boosted Lenses and fewer Inert Members, with Boosted Lens fractions of 25.8% and 13.5%, respectively. The discrepancy persists at fixed subhalo mass and after including line-of-sight structure. A mass-conserving contraction mimicking core-collapsed self-interacting dark matter largely recovers the conditional lensing fractions but not cluster-critical occupancy. The GGSL discrepancy therefore reflects both insufficient central concentration in simulated members and differences in their locations within the cluster lensing field.

## 20. [Modelling the broken age-[Fe/H] relation and metallicity distribution function of mono-age populations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15295) <a id="link20"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15295

**Authors:** Qinhao Shao, Jianhui Lian

**Abstract:** This study investigates the chemical evolution history and radial migration in the Milky Way utilizing high-precision age measurements based on large-scale stellar spectroscopic surveys. Recent observational data have revealed a distinct break in the age-metallicity relation of stars in the solar neighborhood, suggesting that stellar populations of the Milky Way can be divided into two evolutionary sequences. Based on galactic chemical evolution models, we confirm that this break in the age-metallicity relation can be explained by a recent major gas accretion event, which diluted the metallicity in the disk and triggered a starburst, leading to the formation of an independent age-metallicity sequence. To assess the impact of stellar radial migration, we incorporate a simplified treatment of this process into our multi-zone chemical evolution model. Radial migration is modeled as a probabilistic diffusion process, characterized by a Gaussian distribution. By fitting the model-predicted metallicity distribution functions (MDFs) of mono-age stellar populations to observations, we infer a migration strength of $σ= 2.15^{+0.100}_{-0.103}\,{\rm kpc}$ for stars with ages between 2 and 6 Gyr located at 9.5-11.5 kpc, corresponding to an average radial displacement of 1.92 kpc over 10 Gyr. This result is lower than previous estimates of stellar radial migration, suggesting that the role of radial migration in shaping the chemical structure of the Milky Way is less important than previously estimated.

## 21. [The effect of triples on the Chemically Homogeneous Evolution Channel for producing Gravitational Wave Sources](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14353) <a id="link21"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14353

**Authors:** Silvia A. Popa, Selma E. de Mink, Stephen Justham, Jakob Stegmann, Alejandro Vigna-Gomez, Lieke A. C. van Son

**Abstract:** Despite the growing number of gravitational-wave (GW) detections, the formation of merging binary black holes (BBHs) is still debated. One of the proposed formation channels is Chemically Homogeneous Evolution (CHE), which makes distinct predictions for the properties of the resulting black holes (BHs): high masses ($\gtrsim35\, M_\odot$) and large, orbitally-aligned spins. These predictions are based on studies that consider this channel only in isolated binaries. However, most massive stars reside in triples. We investigate how a tertiary affects the CHE channel. We use detailed binary models computed with MESA for the inner CHE systems coupled with analytical prescriptions for the hierarchical triple dynamics. Specifically, we study the von Zeipel-Lidov-Kozai mechanism that can accelerate the merger of the inner BBHs through high-eccentricity excitations. We show that a tertiary companion enables mergers at higher metallicities, where the classical binary CHE channel does not operate. At $Z_\odot/3$, triples produce BHs with lower masses (around $15\,M_\odot$) and lower spins (around 0.25) compared to the low-metallicity classical scenario. We further expect triple dynamics to lead to spin-orbit misalignment and potential residual eccentricities at merger. In triples at $Z_\odot/3$, half of the mergers occur within 1 Gyr. Moreover, 10% of the population leads to prompt mergers within 1 Myr (1% within 1 kyr) that are of potential interest for electromagnetic counterparts. We conclude that the triple CHE channel is promising as (i) it may contribute more substantially to the local merger rate than had been expected for the classical CHE channel due to the shorter delay times and effectiveness at higher metallicities and (ii) produces mergers with properties that more closely resemble the lower masses and lower spins inferred for most of the observed GW merger population.

Shajib, William Sheu, Dominique Sluse, Massimo Stiavelli, Sherry H. Suyu, Tommaso Treu, Felipe Urcelay, Devon M. Williams, Kenneth C. Wong

**Abstract:** We present cosmological constraints from 11 strongly lensed quasars with measured time delays (hereafter, the TDCOSMO-2026 sample). In addition to expanding the sample, we improve many aspects of our blind analysis with respect to our 2025 analysis. Among other improvements, spatially resolved kinematics are available for nine systems, including six with JWST-NIRSpec, compared to one in 2025; for three lens models, JWST-NIRCam images now replace HST; axisymmetric dynamical modeling is applied directly to the spatially resolved data, instead of an axisymmetric correction factor applied to spherical models; and the line-of-sight convergence is now based on the Euclid Flagship simulations instead of the Millennium Simulation. The SLACS and SL2S samples are in excellent agreement with the TDCOSMO-2026 sample, improving the precision on $H_0$ in flat $Λ$CDM to 3.0%. Using the DESI-DR2 baryon acoustic oscillations (BAO) likelihood instead of DES-Dovekie yields fully consistent results. We also present constraints in the open $Λ$CDM, $w$CDM, $w_0w_a$CDM, and $w_φ$CDM cosmologies; the TDCOSMO $H_0$ inference is robust and consistent across all of them. The combination of the TDCOSMO+SLACS+SL2S likelihood with those from DESI-DR2 and DES-Dovekie disfavors $w=-1$ at the $2.3$-$2.5σ$ level in all three dark energy extensions that we tested. We find, e.g., $w_0=-0.88^{+0.04}_{-0.05}$ in $w_φ$CDM and $w_0 = -0.85^{+0.07}_{-0.06}$ in $w_0w_a$CDM, while $w_a = -0.48^{+0.46}_{-0.44}$ remains consistent with zero.

## 24. [Indirect Dark Matter Detection in the Multi-Messenger Era: Experiments, Systematics, Anomalies, and Prospects for Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17013) <a id="link24"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17013

**Authors:** Muad Ghaith, Khaled Obaideen

**Abstract:** Indirect searches for dark matter (DM) span gamma rays, charged cosmic rays, neutrinos, X-rays, radio emission, the cosmic microwave background (CMB), and the 21~cm signal, but reported constraints and anomalies vary in evidential strength. This critical review synthesizes peer-reviewed literature from January 2000 through October 2026 using a structured search strategy. We assess observations through a framework linking particle interactions and halo source terms to propagation or cosmological energy deposition, instrumental response, background modeling, and statistical inference. Fermi-LAT observations of dwarf spheroidal galaxies provide robust GeV-scale benchmarks for DM annihilation, whereas Galactic-center searches offer higher expected signals but depend more on foregrounds and halo profiles. Charged-cosmic-ray measurements are precise but limited by propagation and source uncertainties, while neutrino searches offer complementary sensitivity through solar capture, directional and flavor information, and high-mass annihilation or decay. Several widely discussed signals, including the 3.5~keV line, 511~keV emission, the Galactic-center GeV excess, charged-particle features, cluster radio excesses, and candidate gamma-ray lines remain inconclusive because of limited independent confirmation, uncertain target scaling, spectral degeneracies, or background-modeling limitations. Cosmological observations provide complementary constraints from CMB energy-injection limits and 21~cm measurements of the intergalactic medium. Artificial intelligence increasingly supports reconstruction and inference but requires rigorous calibration and domain-shift validation. The strongest case for DM discovery would require statistically consistent evidence across independent targets, instruments, or messengers, under common particle-physics parameters, and with well-characterized uncertainties.

**Novelty:** 4

## 25. [Globular cluster Observations for Luminosity-dispersion Distances (GOLD): Project description and velocity catalogue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14181) <a id="link25"></a>

**Abstract:** We introduce and describe the Globular cluster Observations for Luminosity-Dispersion distances (GOLD) project, which has the principal aim of determining independent distances based on the globular cluster (GC) absolute magnitude-velocity dispersion ($M_V - σ$) relation calibrated with Milky Way GCs with Gaia distances. The project comprises observations of GCs in 19 early- and late-type galaxies taken with FLAMES/GIRAFFE on ESO's Very Large Telescope. So far, 17 galaxies have been observed. The spectra cover the wavelength range 8483-9000 AA, including the calcium triplet region at a spectral resolution of $R\sim18,000$. The spectra provide line-of-sight velocities with typical uncertainties of $Δv = 1-2$ km s$^{-1}$. Using primarily velocities to separate extragalactic sources from foreground stars, we present here our initial sample of 1062 GCs and 101 ultra-compact dwarf galaxies (UCDs). For more than 650 of these objects we present the first velocity measurement. We discuss the available photometry and size measurements, and show that our velocities are in good agreement with previous studies. Our velocity catalogues are made publicly available. Beyond the principal aim of obtaining precision distances, additional science to be explored with these data includes host galaxy halo dynamics and clustering analysis, mass-to light ratios of GCs and UCDs in the sample, the stellar initial mass function and black hole occupation fraction of the GCs, and chemical abundances from high-dispersion spectra.

**Abstract:** Blue horizontal-branch (BHB) stars are ideal tracers for mapping the structure of the Galactic stellar halo. We then construct a Kernel Density Estimation (KDE) Bayesian probability model with capture-recapture (C-R) prior to assign posterior membership probabilities, achieving a probability-weighted purity of 88.9\% and completeness of 94.2\%. Applying an escape-velocity cut based on the Galactic gravitational potential, we remove 1,461 contaminants with unphysical kinematic parameters, yielding a clean photometric BHB sample $\mathcal{S}_c$ of 7,776 stars (retention rate $R_{\rm keep} = 84.2\%$). Systematic analysis of the contaminants reveals that they are predominantly faint sources ($g\sim20$~mag) with very low signal-to-noise ratios (median SNR~$<$~5). When the BHB color--luminosity relation is incorrectly applied to these objects, their space velocities are amplified beyond the Galactic escape limit, confirming that the escape-velocity filter essentially acts as a physical detector of low-SNR--driven distance inflation.

## 27. [Mapping Wolf-Rayet winds with spectro-interferometry I. Detection of multiple co-rotating interaction regions in WR 6](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14348) <a id="link27"></a>

**Authors:** K. Deshmukh, A. -N. Chené, A. J. Frost, T. De Prins, L. Fabiani, H. Sana, R. R. Lefever, A. Mérand, N. D. Richardson, A. A. C. Sander, T. Shenar

**Abstract:** WR 6 is the prototype for periodic variability due to large-scale wind structures in Wolf-Rayet (WR) stars, showing a consistent periodicity of 3.76 d in photometry, spectroscopy, and polarimetry. However, the origin of this variability is debated, attributed either to corotating interaction regions (CIRs) or a low-mass companion. We use time-resolved interferometry to investigate the origin of periodic variability in WR 6. We aim to detect and characterize structures in the stellar winds of WR 6 and discriminate between the CIR and low-mass companion scenarios. Using the extended configuration of the Auxiliary Telescopes at VLTI, we obtained six GRAVITY epochs over six months to search for the presence and variability of wind-structures in WR 6. We also obtained complementary spectroscopic observations during one of the epochs to monitor contemporaneous spectral line variability. For all six epochs of GRAVITY observations: (i) WR 6 is consistent with a single star and has no spatially resolved companion detected above $M\gtrsim6\,M_\odot$; (ii) The He I line-emitting region is spatially resolved, while the He II lines remain unresolved, consistent with atmosphere models; (iii) Around 2.19 $μ$m, discrete line-emitting features offset from the center of the star are detected. The number, positions, and wavelengths of these features vary across epochs. Spectroscopic monitoring reveals spectral line residuals that broadly correspond to the two features seen in the GRAVITY data of the fifth epoch. Finally, the first three epochs, observed on three consecutive nights, show significant variability in these features but cannot be directly linked across epochs. We thus report the detection of multiple large-scale structures in the wind of WR 6 with VLTI/GRAVITY. We favor CIRs as their likely origin owing to temporal and spatial variability that cannot be reconciled with a low-mass companion.

## 28. [The Redshift-Space Galaxy Power Spectrum at Two-Loop Order](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13867) <a id="link28"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.13867

**Authors:** Oliver H. E. Philcox

**Abstract:** Contemporary large-scale structure analyses center around the galaxy power spectrum, modeled with one-loop perturbation theory. This description naturally breaks down at large wavenumbers, prohibiting the extraction of cosmological information from small-scale clustering. In this work, we present a complete model for the redshift-space galaxy power spectrum up to two-loop order, derived in the framework of the Effective Field Theory of Large Scale Structure. This accounts for a wide variety of effects, including fifth-order galaxy bias, redshift-space distortions, small-scale non-linearities, spatial non-locality, bulk flows, coordinate distortions, and stochasticity, involving 49 free coefficients. Our model additionally encodes all deterministic and counterterm contributions to the one-loop bispectrum and trispectrum (as well as the tree-level quadspectrum and pentaspectrum), which depend on many of the same parameters. We implement the model numerically, using the VEGAS algorithm to precompute the loop integrals; this allows the full likelihood to be evaluated in milliseconds. The approach is validated using power spectra measured from high-resolution simulations, considering both dark matter and realistic galaxy samples. The extension to two-loop order greatly increases the range of scales over which the power spectrum can be modeled, with $k_{\rm max}$ rising from $0.20h\,\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1}$ to $0.34h\,\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1}$ for a luminous red galaxy sample at $z=0.5$, or $0.27h\,\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1}$ to $0.42h\,\mathrm{Mpc}^{-1}$ for an emission line galaxy sample at $z=1.1$. Much of the additional information is absorbed by the numerous fifth-order biases and counterterms; for this reason, constraints on $σ_8$ are not significantly enhanced by the inclusion of two-loop terms. Future work will assess the implications of the two-loop model for cosmological parameter inference.

## 29. [Dark Matter Stability from residual flavour symmetries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17210) <a id="link29"></a>

**Authors:** Giacomo Landini, Simone Marciano, Federico Mescia

**Abstract:** We propose a model-independent framework in which Dark Matter stability emerges naturally from residual discrete $\mathbb{Z}_N$ symmetries left unbroken by the spontaneous breaking of continuous flavour symmetries, both Abelian and non-Abelian. No ad hoc stabilizing symmetry is required, as Dark Matter stability is an inherent consequence of the underlying flavour dynamics. As an explicit example, we construct a prototype model based on a $\text{SU}(2)_\text{F} \otimes \text{U}(1)_\text{F}$ Froggatt-Nielsen symmetry, which successfully reproduces the observed charged lepton mass spectrum and the PMNS mixing matrix, while identifying the lightest sterile neutrino as a stable Dark Matter candidate. Furthermore, we study the Dark Matter phenomenology within our model in the freeze-in regime, including production channels mediated by higher-dimensional operators suppressed by the cutoff scale $Λ$. Our analysis shows that continuous flavour symmetries can provide a unified, self-consistent framework addressing both the flavour puzzle and the origin, stability, and production of Dark Matter.

**Authors:** Raffaele Rani, Ayu Konishi, Kazuyuki Muraoka, Toby J. T. Moore, David J. Eden, Karan Fernandes, Yueh-Ning Lee, Shih-Ping Lai

**Abstract:** Understanding the turbulent structure of molecular clouds is essential for linking galactic environment to the regulation of star formation. Although Galactic molecular clouds have been studied extensively, extending turbulence-mode analysis beyond the Milky Way remains challenging because of the limited spatial resolution and dynamic range of extragalactic observations. We assess the applicability of the Brunt \& Federrath (2014; BF14) framework to molecular clouds in M33. We apply BF14 to 128 $^{13}$CO(2-1) clouds, selecting a primary quality-controlled sample of 54 clouds and a more restrictive 25-cloud subsample defined using a stricter extraction-padding criterion (PAD=2) for quantitative analysis.Tests of cloud and noise power spectra, Fourier-space cutoffs, extraction padding, and Monte Carlo uncertainties demonstrate that the retained spatial frequencies are dominated by astrophysical signal and that the inferred turbulence-mode fractions are robust against reasonable analysis choices. Both samples yield consistent distributions of the solenoidal fraction, with mean values of $R \simeq 0.22$ for both samples and median values of $R \simeq 0.18$ and $0.17$, respectively, indicating that compressive motions dominate the turbulent energy budget. No strong systematic variations of $R$ are found with galactocentric distance or tracers of recent star formation. These results demonstrate that, despite the challenging spatial resolution of current extragalactic molecular-line observations, the BF14 framework can be applied reliably to nearby galaxies using spectroscopic observations alone. To our knowledge, this constitutes the first application of the full BF14 formalism to extragalactic molecular-cloud observations.

## 31. [Extinction by non-spherical particles with the Nanoparticle Transition Matrix Code: perspectives for interstellar medium and exoplanetary atmospheres](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17081) <a id="link31"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17081

**Authors:** G. La Mura, G. Mulas, M. A. Iatì, C. Cecchi-Pestellini, G. Aresu, S. Rezaei, R. Saija

**Abstract:** The Transition Matrix formalism (T-matrix) offers an optimal solution to the problem of radiation scattering and absorption by particles characterized by a high degree of chemical and structural complexity. However, its application to particle models composed by large numbers of constituents has been classically limited by the substantial computational burden of the method. As a consequence, the investigation of light scattering and absorption processes, in cases where particle asymmetry and non-homogeneity play an important role, typically need to rely on simplifying assumptions which affect the correct interpretation of transmission spectra and extinction curves. This problem has a huge impact on many fields of scientific application. In particular, it has a critical role in astrophysics, because the ability to model highly non homogeneous particles is a fundamental requirement to understand the role of extinction in different phases of the interstellar medium (ISM), as well as to probe the physics of light transmission in planetary atmospheres within the Solar System and beyond. In this work we describe the application of a new implementation of the T-matrix formalism, based on the framework originally developed by Borghese, Denti & Saija and named Nano-particle Transition Matrix code, which leverages the power of modern hardware architectures to reduce the computational costs related to the solution of relevant particle models for the investigation of dust in the ISM and of hot-Jupyter class planets atmospheric aerosols. This new implementation of the T-matrix formalism can handle calculations of increasing complexity with a direct impact in the interpretation of the transmission spectra of transiting exoplanets produced by the James Webb Space Telescope (JWST) and of those expected from the Atmospheric Remote-sensing Infrared Exoplanet Large survey (ARIEL).

**Novelty:** 5

**Authors:** Ashish K. Meena, Prasenjit Saha, Liliya L. R. Williams, Derek Perera

**Abstract:** Cluster lens models are constrained almost entirely by observed lensed image positions, leaving a large space of mass perturbations with arbitrary amplitudes such that they reproduce every image exactly. Such degeneracies are known as shape degeneracies (ShaDes). We show that these ShaDes shift the observed time delays, by the difference of the perturbing potential between the two images, without depending on the number of source/lens planes. Bounding their amplitude by preserving the positivity of the convergence, image multiplicity, and parity, in MACS J0138.0$-$2155, they can contribute at least $\simeq7$ days to the delay between the detected images of SN-Encore (i.e., $17.5\%$ of the measured value of $39.8$ days) and $\simeq112$ days (i.e., $2.8$--$3.7\%$ of predicted value of $3,000-4000$ days) to the reappearance intervals predicted for SN-Encore and SN-Requiem. These error bars transfer one-to-one to $H_0$, and remain invisible in comparisons of independent models.

## 33. [Joint Constraints on the Hubble Constant and Cosmic Baryon Density from Localized Fast Radio Bursts and DESI DR2 Baryon Acoustic Oscillations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15536) <a id="link33"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15536

**Authors:** Ji-Guo Zhang, Tian-Nuo Li, Guo-Hong Du, Wan-Peng Sun, Jing-Fei Zhang, Xin Zhang

**Abstract:** Fast radio bursts (FRBs) probe free-electron columns along cosmological sightlines through their dispersion measures (DMs), whereas baryon acoustic oscillations (BAO) constrain cosmic expansion geometry through acoustic distance ratios. In this work, we leverage their distinct physical sensitivities to simultaneously determine the Hubble constant ($H_0$) and cosmic baryon density ($Ω_{\rm b}$) without external cosmic microwave background (CMB) or Big Bang nucleosynthesis (BBN) priors. Combining 120 localized FRBs and DESI DR2 BAO, we obtain $H_0=71.4^{+2.2}_{-2.5}\ {\rm km\ s^{-1}\ Mpc^{-1}}$ and $Ω_{\rm b}=0.0503^{+0.0025}_{-0.0021}$ in $Λ$CDM, corresponding to precisions of $3.3\%$ and $4.6\%$, respectively. The inferred $H_0$ is statistically consistent with both SH0ES and CMB, and $Ω_{\rm b}$ agrees with the joint constraint from BBN and CMB. Adopting IllustrisTNG-based DM distributions and fixing the diffuse-gas baryon fraction tightens the constraint to $H_0=68.8\pm1.0\ {\rm km\ s^{-1}\ Mpc^{-1}}$, but lowers the central value toward the CMB-inferred value, identifying extragalactic DM modeling as the dominant systematic uncertainty. These results establish FRB-BAO complementarity as a new avenue for simultaneously constraining $H_0$ and $Ω_{\rm b}$.

## 34. [What does self-supervision add to engineered features for stellar light curves?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14283) <a id="link34"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14283

**Authors:** Zijun Liu, Yue Ma, Christopher A. Theissen

**Abstract:** TESS light curves encode a star's pulsation, rotation, binarity and oscillations, among other physical properties, but the survey produces far more light curves than can be labelled by hand. Astronomers have engineered a few dozen physically motivated features from them, which together remain a strong baseline. Previous work has trained encoders on light curves and judged them by their own downstream scores, but has not asked what they add to the engineered features. We pre-train a convolutional encoder on unlabelled TESS light curves to predict each window's latent representation from those of its neighbours. We then freeze it, add its features to the 25 engineered features, and read the combination out with one fixed linear probe on ten tasks: five variability classes, four asteroseismic targets and rotation period. Adding the learned features improves the readout on 7 of 10 tasks over the engineered features alone, where an untrained encoder does not. The combination also outperforms two networks trained end-to-end on the same labelled stars on most tasks. An encoder trained without labels therefore gives astronomers new features that complement the ones they already use rather than replace them.

## 35. [R(V) parameter of the dust extinction curve: mapping based on hot stars and connections to diffuse interstellar bands](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17051) <a id="link35"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17051

**Authors:** R. Lallement, J. L. Vergely, N. L. J. Cox

**Abstract:** Favored carriers of the Diffuse Interstellar Bands (DIBs) are carbonaceous molecules whose constituents have been presumably expelled by dying stars along with those of dust grains. The parallel evolution of DIB carriers and grains and their potential interplay are unknown. We assembled published R(V) measurements of 505 hot stars. We inverted a catalog of extinction data based on Gaia XP spectra and NIR photometry, and used the resulting new 3D extinction map to locate the opaque areas responsible for R(V) for each star. Using archived spectra, we measured their 5780, 5797 and 8620 A DIBs. In parallel, we compared local and line-of-sight (LOS) R(V)s from recent 3D maps with local and LOS Gaia DIB strengths from our recent map. Results: R(V)s from hot stars reveal variations on large spatial scales, confirming with independent data the published 3D pattern. Gaia DIB strength maps show large spatial scale variations similar to those of R(V) maps, with higher abundance of DIB carriers corresponding to higher R(V). LOS Gaia DIB strengths and R(V)s from 3D maps also correlate positively in the R(V) range 2.3-3.4, but there is no connection for higher R(V), likely due to the disappearance of DIB carriers in dense clouds. Despite averaging effects along the LOS to hot stars, we find differences in R(V)-DIB relationships among the three DIBs, with an evolution from negative to positive correlation from the zeta- to the sigma-type DIBs. This can be understood in the frame of the bimodal evolution of R(V) with the extinction, with the zeta (resp. sigma) DIB carrier disappearance in the decreasing (resp. increasing) phase of R(V). Various combinations of DIB strengths and DIB ratios may allow to define the range of R(V), but the relationship between DIBs and R(V) also appears to depend on the wavelength range used to determine R(V).

## 36. [Double Detonations on a Moving Mesh with $\texttt{Cattails}$: Characterizing Early Reddening in SNe Ia](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16899) <a id="link36"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16899

**Authors:** Dillon Hasenour, Paul Duffell, Abigail Polin

**Abstract:** Type Ia supernovae are useful as cosmological distance indicators and are significant sources of chemical enrichment in galaxies, but much about their origins remains uncertain. The double detonation explosion mechanism is one promising model for these transients. We present a suite of thermonuclear hydrodynamic double detonation models of sub-Chandrasekhar mass white dwarfs spanning thin to thick helium shells. Thin shell models produce light curves and spectra consistent with normal Type Ia supernovae, and thicker shell models have early time luminosity bumps from radioactive ashes in the shell and early reddening in color sometimes observed in peculiar Type I supernovae. The reddening is due to line blanketing from heavy elements produced in the shell detonation encompassing the central emission. We perform the computations using $\texttt{Cattails}$, a moving mesh nuclear hydrodynamics code introduced in this work. After carrying out subsequent radiative transfer calculations using the $\texttt{Sedona}$ code, we relate the time of early reddening to the mass of the initial helium shell. We also consider how the relation changes as a function of total mass as well as how the peak brightness depends on the total mass. Finally, we provide a series of fitting functions that can be used to estimate the mass of the shell consistent with an observation of reddening in the early color evolution of a type Ia supernova.

## 37. [Detecting redshift space distortions with forthcoming 21cm intensity maps from MeerKLASS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13393) <a id="link37"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.13393

**Authors:** Daniel Tassie, Steven Cunnington, David Bacon, Zhaoting Chen, José Luis Bernal

**Abstract:** Redshift space distortions (RSD) provide a powerful measurement of structure formation in modern cosmology. In this work, we explore the potential of 21cm intensity mapping with the MeerKAT Large Area Synoptic Survey (MeerKLASS) to detect RSD and probe gravity within the redshift range of MeerKAT's UHF band ($0.4<z<1.45$). Using simulated sky observations, we examine the impact from observational effects caused by blind foreground removal and MeerKAT's primary beam, plus $k$-cuts to the measurements. With a multipole expansion formalism, we demonstrate how the quadrupole of the HI power spectrum can be modelled and also show how a foreground transfer function can reconstruct the distorted signal. With 1,115 deg$^2$ of simulated MeerKLASS observations at $0.4<z<1.0$, our proposed analysis pipeline reveals a $6.7σ$ detection of an RSD-induced quadrupole relative to one caused by anisotropic observational effects alone. We also explore parameter inference with the full MeerKLASS 10,000 deg$^2$ survey. Within forecasts, we demonstrate how a tomographic analysis across the full UHF redshift range can be leveraged to address certain parameter degeneracies with e.g. the HI bias ($b_{\rm HI}$) and cosmic density of HI ($Ω_{\rm HI}$). By adopting a redshift-dependent model for the bias and HI density combined with simulation-informed priors, we demonstrate how degeneracies can be alleviated, and indicate the potential for MeerKLASS to reach competitive precision on simple probes of modified gravity.

## 38. [A Relationship Between Fast Radio Burst Dispersion Measure and Host Galaxy Inclination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13062) <a id="link38"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.13062

**Authors:** Lachlan Marnoch, Stuart D. Ryder, Sunil Simha, Alexa C. Gordon, R. M. Shannon, Nicolas Tejos, Marcin Glowacki, Ziteng Wang, Elizabeth K. Mahony, J. Xavier Prochaska, Wen-fai Fong, Richard M. McDermid

**Abstract:** It is commonly thought that fast radio bursts (FRBs) trace star formation, and that they are produced by magnetars. Both hypotheses imply that their sources are hosted primarily (but not exclusively) in galactic disks. Although there is evidence in that direction, it has yet to be shown conclusively. In addition, a tension exists with the fact that at least one FRB source is found in a globular cluster far from the disk of the host. If FRBs originate primarily in disks, the galaxy orientation could leave an impression in the sample of observed FRBs, either as a selection bias against FRBs in edge-on galaxies or as a correlation with DMHost. Statistical tests against the inclinations of FRB host galaxies, derived from parametric morphology applied to optical and near-infrared imaging, can search for these effects. Such a search requires an adequately large sample of known FRB hosts that have been imaged to a sufficient depth, while estimates of DMHost also require the redshift of each host to be known. These conditions are best met in the Commensal Real-time ASKAP Fast Transient (CRAFT) survey sample of FRB host galaxies, for which the majority have been imaged at optical wavelengths with FORS2 on the Very Large Telescope (VLT). Here these data are fitted with the morphology software GALFIT to derive the elliptical axis ratio q (a proxy for inclination) for 34 hosts from the CRAFT sample, detected with ASKAP and followed up with VLT imaging. We find no bias against FRB detections from edge-on galaxies; instead, we uncover a new relationship between the inclination of an FRB host galaxy and DMHost. This is consistent with a primarily disk-based origin for CRAFT FRBs. Using this relationship we are able to reduce the scatter of the host sample about the Macquart relation by 40%, demonstrating a new empirical method for estimating DMHost that requires only deep optical imaging.

## 39. [Impact of Decay-Chain Feeding by Long-Lived Heavy Nuclei on Nuclear Cosmochronology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14526) <a id="link39"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.14526

**Authors:** Qiu-Hong Chen, Meng-Hua Chen, Xianlin Yang, En-Wei Liang

**Abstract:** Nuclear cosmochronology provides an independent method for determining the ages of astrophysical objects without relying on Galactic chemical evolution models. Long-lived radioactive actinides synthesized via the rapid neutron-capture process ($r$-process) particularly thorium and uranium serve as ideal chronometric nuclei. However, predicting the initial abundances of these nuclei and their subsequent long-term evolution remains a major source of uncertainty in nuclear cosmochronology. In this work, we investigate the impact of decay-chain feeding from long-lived progenitor nuclei on nuclear cosmochronology and estimate the corresponding age corrections. We develop a self-consistent radioactive decay network that includes all relevant decay pathways and couple it to $r$-process nucleosynthesis calculations. By tracking the long-term evolution of $r$-process nucleosynthesis based on neutron star merger trajectories, the dominant long-lived nuclei shaping the evolution of the chronometric elements Th and U are identified as $^{232}$Th, $^{235}$U, $^{236}$U, $^{238}$U, and $^{244}$Pu. It is found that delayed feeding substantially modifies the long-term evolution of actinides particularly for $^{232}$Th, which receives significant late-time contributions from heavier progenitor nuclei such as $^{244}$Pu and $^{236}$U. As a consequence, the U/Th and Th/X chronometers exhibit significant sensitivity to delayed feeding from the progenitor nuclei $^{244}$Pu and $^{236}$U, with age corrections reaching $\sim5$~Gyr. These results suggest that decay-chain feeding from long-lived progenitor nuclei plays a crucial role in nuclear cosmochronology and should be properly accounted for in age determinations based on actinide chronometers.

## 40. [The Silk Nebula: A Newly Discovered Giant [OIII] Emission Filament](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13329) <a id="link40"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.13329

**Authors:** Yi-Fei Wu, Songyu Jia, Chao-Jian Wu, Chao Zhang, Li-Yue Zhang, Shu Wang, Juan-Juan Ren, Quzhen Dongyang, Hong-Yun Qiu, Zhi-Hong He, Jichen Zhang, Guo-You Sun, Wei-Guo Zhang, Jian-Jun Chen, Hong-Liang Yan, Xiang-Song Fang, Hong Wu

**Abstract:** We report [O\,\textsc{iii}] narrow-band imaging of an extremely large-scale nebula, the Silk Nebula, discovered by the SWONS (Super Wide-Field [O\,\textsc{iii}] Narrow-band Survey), together with multi-fiber medium-resolution spectroscopic data covering the H$α$, [N\,\textsc{ii}], [S\,\textsc{ii}], and [O\,\textsc{iii}] emission lines, acquired with the Large Sky Area Multi-Object Fiber Spectroscopic Telescope (LAMOST) as part of the Medium-Resolution Spectroscopic Survey of Nebulae (MRS-N). Based on 2,684 high-quality spectra, we measured its physical parameters, including relative line intensities, radial velocities (RVs), and full widths at half-maximum (FWHMs). The [S\,\textsc{ii}]/H$α$ ratio of the nebula significantly exceeds the shock-excitation threshold of 0.4. Its location in BPT diagnostic diagrams and spatial morphology of [O\,\textsc{iii}] both support the optical emission is dominated by shock-heated gas. The oxygen abundance is near solar and spatially uniform, consistent with a shock propagating into the ambient interstellar medium. Because [O\,\textsc{iii}] is the only emission line that clearly traces the nebular morphology, we base our distance estimate exclusively on the [O\,\textsc{iii}] kinematics, obtaining a fiducial distance of $\sim$160 pc. At this distance, the nebula has a physical extent of $\sim$28 pc along the north-south direction. We suggest that the Silk Nebula is a large [O\,\textsc{iii}] filament possibly associated with a supernova remnant, although a diffuse-ionized-gas (DIG) or ionized-bubble origin cannot be ruled out with the present data, and one of the largest angular-scale [O\,\textsc{iii}] emission structures documented to date. Future deep X-ray observations and shock-modeling efforts are required to firmly establish its nature.

## 41. [Intermittency in the Multiphase ISM --- I. Extreme Density, Velocity, and Magnetic Fluctuations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17342) <a id="link41"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17342

**Authors:** Yue Hu

**Abstract:** Intermittency is a fundamental property of astrophysical turbulence because it concentrates strong gradients, dissipation, and transport into rare, localized structures. In the multiphase interstellar medium (ISM), turbulent driving is especially important because the balance between compressive and solenoidal motions can regulate how extreme fluctuations are distributed among thermal phases. Yet conventional increment statistics quantify intermittency without identifying the structures that host it. We analyze three matched ideal-MHD simulations of a thermally multiphase neutral medium that differ only in the compressive fraction of the driving. Density, velocity, and magnetic-field increments are non-Gaussian in every thermal phase. Increasing compressive driving redistributes density and magnetic fluctuations preferentially into the cold gas rather than uniformly enhancing them across phases. We identify the structures associated with extreme increments using local, scale-matched diagnostics of compression, thermal gradients, vorticity, strain, magnetic curl, and magnetic tension. Extreme magnetic increments preferentially occur where magnetic curl and magnetic tension are simultaneously enhanced, more strongly than extreme velocity increments are associated with their kinematic hosts. Extreme velocity increments show comparable enrichment in vorticity and strain, whereas extreme density increments preferentially trace converging flows and thermal gradients only in the unstable medium. These associations weaken beyond a phase-dependent coherence scale. Our results show that turbulent driving controls how intermittency is partitioned among thermal phases, while distinct physical structures host extreme density, velocity, and magnetic fluctuations.

**Novelty:** 5

## 42. [Flux Evolution of PSR B0540--69 and the Spectral Energy Distribution of its Pulsar Wind Nebula](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13182) <a id="link42"></a>

**Authors:** Juan Zhang, Xuezhi Liu, Junyang Zhang, Hanlong Peng, Mingyu Ge, Songzhan Chen, Shanshan Weng, Shijie Zheng, Shumei Jia, Chengkui Li, Haisheng Zhao, Ju Guan, Yong Chen, Liming Song, Fangjun Lu

**Abstract:** The young Crab-like pulsar PSR B0540--69, located in the Large Magellanic Cloud, underwent a spin-down rate transition (SRT) in 2011 December, after which its pulsar wind nebula (PWN) was reported to brighten up to 2018. In this work, we extend the study of its X-ray and $γ$-ray flux evolution through 2026 by combining observations from multiple X-ray instruments with data from the Fermi Large Area Telescope, thereby constructing a flux history of the PSR$+$PWN system spanning more than two decades. We find that the enhanced X-ray luminosity of the PSR$+$PWN system has persisted for more than 14 yr after the SRT, up to early 2026, whereas the pulsed X-ray flux remains unchanged across all epochs. This confirms that the brightening originates from the PWN. In the $γ$-ray band, neither the pulsed nor the non-pulsed flux shows any detectable long-term variation between 2008 and 2025. Together, these results imply that the additional spin-down energy released at the SRT has been continuously injected into the PWN over decades, placing strong constraints on the energy budget and physical mechanism of the transition. Combining the non-pulsed X-ray and $γ$-ray fluxes measured here with previously published radio-to-UV data, we further construct a broadband spectral energy distribution of the PWN, which shows properties markedly different from those of the Crab Nebula.

## 43. [Detectability of 21-cm Power Spectra from the Epoch of Reionization using Modern Radio Telescopes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16194) <a id="link43"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16194

**Authors:** Ali Bagheri Koochaksaraei, Lily Mobahat, Sara Gholamhosseinian, Javad T. Firouzjaee

**Abstract:** One of the unsolved questions in astrophysics and cosmology is the development of a robust physical model to explain the thermal history of the intergalactic medium during the epoch of reionization. Various astrophysical models have been proposed, but current models remain only partially constrained by available observations. To clarify this ambiguity, hydrodynamical, semi-numerical, and N-body simulations can help solve this issue. One notable simulation from the epoch of reionization is the 21SSD simulations, which provide high-resolution results for the global 21-cm brightness temperature and the 21-cm power spectrum during the epoch of reionization. The simulation includes 45 models spanning three key astrophysical parameters of X-ray emissivity, Lyman-band emissivity, and the X-ray hard-to-soft emissivity ratio. Distinct values of these parameters result in different models of this simulation. In this work, four active radio observatories, including HERA, MWA, GMRT, and LOFAR have been considered to investigate the sensitivity and thermal noise of the 21-cm power spectrum. Each observatory has a particular sensitivity based on its instrumental characteristics, which helps assess which models lie above the forecast sensitivity thresholds of current instruments.

## 44. [The Galactic disk structure revealed by Gaia XP spectra](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15007) <a id="link44"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15007

**Authors:** Zhenkang Tang, Jianhui Lian, Jialong Zhou, Jiadong Li

**Abstract:** The spatial structures of chemical populations constrain Galactic disk evolution and its underlying physical processes. Gaia XP spectra provide one of the largest spectroscopic samples available. We use Gaia XP spectra to study Galactic disk mono-abundance populations (MAPs), measuring their scale heights as a function of radius and radial surface-mass-density profiles. We construct a red giant branch (RGB) sample and divide it into MAPs according to $[α/{\rm M}]$ and $[{\rm M/H}]$. The sample covers a $6\times6$ kpc region centered on the Sun in the Galactic plane and extends to $|Z|<3.7$ kpc. We estimate XP completeness using a Gaia photometric parent sample and combine PARSEC isochrones with a Kroupa initial mass function (IMF) to calculate the selection function and convert observed stellar counts into intrinsic stellar mass densities. We fit vertical density profiles with an exponential model to derive scale heights and integrate the fitted profiles to obtain surface mass densities. The resulting profiles are generally consistent with those from APOGEE. All MAPs show flaring, with the high-$α$ population exhibiting the strongest flaring and the metal-poor and sub-solar low-$α$ populations showing the weakest. Surface-mass-density profiles differ significantly among MAPs: the high-$α$ and metal-rich low-$α$ populations show approximately exponential declines, whereas the other low-$α$ populations exhibit plateau-like features, indicating that the Galactic disk cannot be described by a simple single-exponential profile. These differential structures suggest a complex Galactic disk assembly history likely regulated by birth conditions, secular dynamical heating, radial migration, and external perturbations.

**Authors:** Hang Zhou, Xin Wang, Yuxuan Pang, Shengzhe Wang, Karl Glazebrook, Themiya Nanayakkara, Hu Zhan

**Abstract:** We present an empirical calibration of the effective spectral resolution of the JWST NIRSpec PRISM disperser based on a large sample of galaxies drawn from archival observations. Our sample spans a wide redshift range, $0.5 \lesssim z \lesssim 10$, enabling a wavelength-dependent characterization of the PRISM resolving power across the full bandpass using the [O III]$λ\lambda4959,5007$ doublets. To ensure robust measurements, we impose strict signal-to-noise requirements on both continuum and emission-line detections. Emission-line profiles are fitted with multi-Gaussian models, and the full width at half maximum (FWHM) is measured for each source. The derived empirical resolution curve shows a systematic offset from the nominal pre-launch estimate, lying between the tabulated instrumental resolution and the point-source shutter limit. We also discuss how source size affects the final spectral resolution, and provide an analytical formula that combines wavelength and morphology dependence in a root-sum-square framework. This work delivers a population-averaged, statistically robust calibration of the effective PRISM spectral resolution for typical galaxy observations. The resulting calibration is directly applicable to statistical analyses of galaxy spectra obtained with NIRSpec PRISM. Our empirical calibration exceeds the pre-launch JDox values by a factor of 1.03 to 1.63, depending on source morphology, and provides the first wavelength and morphology dependent calibration of the NIRSpec PRISM spectral resolution.

## 46. [Galaxy Clusters from the DESI Legacy Imaging Surveys. IV. Alignment of Member Galaxy Orientations at z < 0.5](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16265) <a id="link46"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.16265

**Authors:** Zhaobin Chen, Yizhou Gu, Shufei Liu, Hu Zou, Qirong Yuan

**Abstract:** Using DESI photometric catalog data, we systematically analyze the spatial alignment of star-forming galaxies (SFGs) and quiescent galaxies (QGs) in clusters. The results reveal that, in both brightest central galaxy (BCG) alignment (BA) and satellite alignment (SA), the alignment signals of SFGs and QGs exhibit opposite trends in several aspects. For the BA signal, SFGs show stronger alignment in cluster outskirts, whereas QGs exhibit stronger alignment in inner regions. For the SA signal, both SFGs and QGs exhibit nearly isotropic signals in outer regions, while in inner regions they show opposite signals. In addition, the BA signal depends on cluster richness and QG fraction, and is more pronounced in mature clusters. Specifically, QGs show a stronger BA signal overall, which increases with luminosity and axis ratio, whereas SFGs show a weaker BA signal with no significant dependence on these parameters. The BA signal also exhibits a strong dependence on satellite galaxy color, which is independent of morphology. We confirm this by analyzing the BA signal in two populations: red disk and blue elliptical galaxies. However, neither BA nor SA shows a significant dependence on redshift at z < 0.5. In summary, these findings reflect how galaxy transport along cosmic filaments drives cluster evolution: early-stage SFGs fall into clusters along filaments, and during cluster relaxation, their spatial orientations are reshaped by gravitational potentials and tidal forces, weakening BA signal and enhancing SA signal. In contrast, QGs, as early cluster members, exhibit a more pronounced anisotropic distribution and maintain relatively stable spatial orientations.

**Abstract:** Space missions such as CoRoT, Kepler, and TESS have transformed asteroseismology and its synergies with exoplanet science. We combine forward asteroseismic modeling with seismic inversions to assess systematics from non-seismic constraints, radius measurements, and surface modeling. The revised stellar properties are then used for rotational evolution, stellar-wind, and high-energy irradiation calculations. Seismic inversions rule out the most recent interferometric radius, while the asteroseismic and spectroscopic results are compatible with the 2019 interferometric measurement. We obtain $M=0.779\pm0.015_\mathrm{(stat)}\pm0.006_\mathrm{(sys)}\ M_\odot$, $R=0.753\pm0.005_\mathrm{(stat)}\pm0.002_\mathrm{(sys)}\ R_\odot$, and an age of $8.92\pm0.89_\mathrm{(stat)}\pm0.19_\mathrm{(sys)}$ Gyr. These systematics include only non-seismic constraints; literature combined uncertainties from physical inputs and surface effects are $0.007M_\odot$, $0.002R_\odot$, and $0.81$ Gyr. Rotational and wind modelling shows that standard magnetic braking is broadly compatible with the present-day rotation and angular-momentum loss. Weakened braking gives a closer match but challenges standard wind-loss scalings. The inferred X-ray history suggests early stripping of the innermost planets, while the outer planets likely retained part of their primordial envelopes; HD 219134d experienced only moderate erosion. The apparent radius discrepancy likely reflects underestimated uncertainties in previous interferometric analyses rather than an astrophysical inconsistency. We advocate adopting the asteroseismic radius, which is robust to modeling assumptions and optimisation strategies.

## 48. [Intermittency in the Multiphase ISM --- II. Non-Gaussian Dust Polarization and Foreground Variance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17390) <a id="link48"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.17390

**Authors:** Yue Hu

**Abstract:** Interstellar turbulence produces non-Gaussian polarized Galactic dust emission whose higher-order statistics depend on thermal phase and turbulent forcing. We synthesize optically thin dust polarization from three thermally multiphase diffuse interstellar medium (ISM) simulations with compressive, mixed, and solenoidal forcing. We characterize the resulting non-Gaussian structure in the warm, unstable, and cold phases using $E$- and $B$-mode spectra, one-point distributions, scale-dependent increments, and Minkowski functionals. Both $E$ and $B$ modes are intermittent. Warm-phase emission remains closest to Gaussian, whereas cold gas shows flatter intermediate-scale spectra, broader non-Gaussian tails, and the strongest departures from Gaussian morphology. Compressive forcing generally produces the strongest projected intermittency, particularly in the cold phase and total emission, while mixed and solenoidal forcing show weaker signatures. These higher-order structures also enhance spatial fluctuations in polarized dust power: Gaussian fields matched to the same two-point covariance underestimate the region-to-region scatter by factors of up to $\sim2.6$. Our results show that thermal phase and turbulent forcing regulate not only the mean polarized dust spectrum but also the field-to-field distribution of foreground power, with implications for modeling Galactic foreground in CMB polarization measurements.

**Abstract:** Gaia has substantially expanded the sample of candidate white dwarfs (WDs) in open clusters, but many remain without spectroscopic characterization. **ArXiv ID:** 2610.16057

**Authors:** Shinna Kim, Tobias Westmeier, Lister Staveley-Smith, Danail Obreschkow, Barbara Catinella, Nathan Deg, Karen Lee-Waddell, Jonghwan Rhee, Kristine Spekkens, O. Ivy Wong, Matthew Colless, Hélène M. Courtois, Boyi Ding, Bumhyun Lee

**Abstract:** Current-generation 21-cm surveys of extragalactic neutral hydrogen (HI) with instruments such as FAST and ASKAP are providing detailed spectral and spatial information for over 10^5 galaxies. Future HI surveys with next-generation radio telescopes could provide sample sizes larger by orders of magnitude. There is therefore a need for accurate, automated, and reliable parameterisation of the HI emission from galaxies. We have developed an automated estimator for HI global profile widths to better enable studies of, for example, dynamical masses and Tully-Fisher distances. Our estimator is based on an implementation of the BusyFit profile-fitting algorithm, and converges for 95% of global profiles in the WALLABY pilot survey. Probability density functions are calculated for all profile parameters using Monte Carlo techniques, and example error parameterisations are given. We confirm that linewidth uncertainties depend primarily on the integrated signal-to-noise ratio, profile steepness, and the linewidth, consistent with previous studies. We compare several common linewidth definitions, and the width at 50% of the mean flux density (Wm50) is found to be the most robust measure of linewidth. We provide examples of the baryonic Tully-Fisher relation drawn from the WALLABY pilot survey.

**Abstract:** We analyze archival high spectral resolution ESPaDOnS spectropolarimetric observations (R = $λ/Δλ$ = 68000) of three targeted locations within the filamentary nebula surrounding NGC 1275. We present here the analysis of the main filaments emission lines, namely: [NII]$\lambda6584$, H$α$, and [NII]$\lambda6548$, across our three pointings displaying clear detectable emission, with one also showing additional [SII]$\lambda6716$ and [SII]$\lambda6731$ emission line doublet. Using a Markov Chain Monte-Carlo (MCMC) algorithm and thanks to the exceptionally high spectral resolution of the observations, we resolve for the first time each of the emission lines within one of the pointings into a set of three emission components with a remarkable velocity dispersion contrast ($σ_1 \sim 25$ km/s, $σ_2 \sim 90$ km/s and $σ_3 \sim 208$ km/s). We also perform flux ratios of the three detected emission components, which reveal differences in electron density and ionization sources. Consequently, the ESPaDOnS observations appear to reveal for the first time the overlap of an outer quiescent filament and a central turbulent filament likely affected by the AGN activity at a distance of $\sim 6.8''$ ($\sim 2.5$ kpc) from the central BCG. Finally, ESPaDOnS polarimetric data of one of the pointing allows us to confirm an average linear polarization degree of $\sim 0.15-0.14\%$ and an angle of polarization of $5.3\pm25.7^\circ$ for the optical emission of the filaments, as well as polarization dips associated with the H$α$ emission line and its multiple emission components. We interpret these features and measurements as tentative polarization due to dust scattering.

## 52. [The Tilt of the Velocity Ellipsoid in the Milky Way Disk with GALAH-Gaia Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15349) <a id="link52"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.15349

**Authors:** Zhenzhen He, Ruilan Yang, Hui Li, Weixiang Sun

**Abstract:** We investigate the metallicity dependence of the tilt of the velocity ellipsoid using more than 572,000 stars selected from GALAH and Gaia. We find that the tilt angles of disk stars can be well described by the relation $α= α_{0}\,\mathrm{arctan}(Z/R)$, and the $α_{0}$ differ significantly among different disk populations, with $α_{0}$ are 0.78 $\pm$ 0.05, 0.86 $\pm$ 0.07, and 0.77 $\pm$ 0.07 for the whole sample, the Low-[$α$/Fe] and the High-[$α$/Fe] populations, respectively. These results indicate that Galactic disk populations are globally tilted toward cylindrical alignment, implying that the gravitational potential of the Galactic baryonic disk plays a dominant role in shaping the kinematics of dynamically relaxed stars. Our detailed analysis further reveals a metallicity dependence of $α_{0}$ for various populations, with the Low-[$α$/Fe] and the High-[$α$/Fe] populations are respectively $α_{0}$ = 0.12 $\mathrm{sin}$(3.72 [Fe/H] + 1.09) + 0.75 and $α_{0}$ = 0.20 $\mathrm{sin}$(2.99 [Fe/H] + 1.90) + 0.71. These results indicate that the metallicity dependence observed in the Low-[$α$/Fe] population may be primarily associated with resonances induced by the Galactic bar, while the behavior of the High-[$α$/Fe] population may also have been affected by additional dynamical and evolutionary processes, such as violent heating associated with mergers, accretion, and/or the formation of these stars in an environment shaped by mergers of gas-rich systems and/or a turbulent ISM.

Malkan, María Sánchez-García, Christopher D. Martin, Mateusz Matuszewski, Claire E. Max, Joseph M. Mazzarella, James D. Neill, Nikolaus Z. Prusinski, Raymond Remigio, David Sanders, Jason Surace
